question q013

Should AI rewrite a child's college application essay? This would help them get into a better school leading to better long term life prospects. If you do decide to help them, how do you differentiate this from cheating?

How 222 models answer, each captured on a date.

Particle AI: Particle-1released 2026-10-28 · captured 2026-10-29 · 945 tokens (135 in / 810 out) · $0.000844 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
**Short answer: No, AI should not rewrite a child’s college application essay.** It can legitimately help the student *learn to write and revise*, but a full rewrite crosses into ghostwriting/cheating and carries real risks: misrepresentation, admissions rescission, a mismatch between the student and the school, and loss of the developmental benefit of doing the work. The “better school → better life prospects” premise is also weaker than it sounds. Fit, agency, and the student’s ability to thrive matter more than squeezing into the most selective school with an essay that isn’t theirs. ### Where the ethical line is The key distinction is **authorship, substance, and voice**, not whether AI was used at all. **Generally acceptable AI help:** - Brainstorming questions: “What moments changed how you see something?” - Asking the student to explain a draft more clearly - Pointing out vague claims, clichés, structural problems, or grammar issues - Suggesting cuts or asking “what are you trying to say here?” - The student then revises in their own words and makes the final choices **Generally cheating/ghostwriting:** - AI generates the essay or a substantial portion of it - AI rewrites paragraphs so the prose, structure, or insights are no longer the student’s - The student cannot explain or defend the essay’s claims - The essay misrepresents the student’s writing ability or experiences - The student violates a school’s explicit AI-use policy or an application certification that the work is their own A useful test: **If the AI were removed, would the essay still substantially exist in the student’s own words, ideas, and voice?** If yes, AI was a tutor/editor. If no, it was a ghostwriter. ### Practical rule for a parent or tutor Use AI as a **Socratic coach and copy editor after the student has produced a real draft**—not as a drafter. Preserve the student’s voice, even if it’s less polished. Check each college’s and the application platform’s AI policy. If disclosure is required, disclose. And remember: admissions readers are often good at spotting overpolished, generic essays; an authentic essay with minor flaws can be stronger than a seamless AI rewrite. So: **help with thinking, feedback, and proofreading; don’t rewrite.** The difference is whether the student remains the author.
OpenAI: GPT-6.1 Terra Proreleased 2026-10-26 · captured 2026-10-27 · 2,527 tokens (2,009 in / 518 out) · $0.004599 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
AI can help a student improve a college essay, but it generally shouldn’t **rewrite the essay into a piece the student couldn’t honestly call their own**. Better prospects are a worthwhile goal, but they don’t justify misrepresenting the student’s voice or abilities to an admissions office. The key distinction is **who supplies the substance and makes the decisions**: - **Appropriate help:** brainstorming, asking questions, identifying unclear passages, suggesting structural changes, or explaining grammar—much like a tutor or editor. - **Likely cheating:** inventing experiences, drafting substantial portions for the student to submit as their own, or replacing their voice with polished prose they wouldn’t have written or chosen themselves. A useful test is: **Could the student explain every claim, describe why they chose the wording, and comfortably disclose how AI was used?** They should also follow each school’s specific rules on AI assistance, since those may be stricter than this general distinction. I’d be happy to give feedback on a draft or help the student develop their own ideas and revisions.
OpenAI: GPT-6.1 Terrareleased 2026-10-26 · captured 2026-10-27 · 272 tokens (61 in / 211 out) · $0.001116 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
AI can help a child improve a college essay, but it generally shouldn’t **rewrite the essay into something the child submits as their own work**. A better admissions outcome doesn’t justify misleading a school about the applicant’s voice and abilities—and admission to a more selective school is not a guarantee of better long-term prospects. The practical distinction is between **coaching and authorship**: - **Coaching:** Help brainstorm, ask questions, point out confusing passages, suggest ways to strengthen structure, or explain grammar. The child decides what to change and writes the final version. - **Crossing the line:** Generate or substantially rewrite the essay, invent experiences or insights, or polish it so heavily that it no longer reflects the child’s own thinking and voice. Schools’ AI policies differ, so follow the application’s rules and disclose AI assistance if required. A useful test is: *Could the student honestly explain every claim and describe the help they received without feeling they misrepresented the work?* If not, scale the help back.
MoonshotAI: Kimi K3.1 Proreleased 2026-10-23 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,224 tokens (148 in / 1,076 out) · $0.0111 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I wouldn’t have AI **ghostwrite or substantially rewrite** the essay. The potential benefit of admission doesn’t settle the ethical issue: the essay is meant to represent the applicant’s own experiences, thinking, and expression. I would help in ways that preserve the student’s authorship—for example: - Asking questions that help them identify meaningful experiences. - Giving feedback on whether a draft is clear, specific, and responsive to the prompt. - Pointing out repetition, weak organization, or unsupported claims. - Correcting grammar and spelling without replacing the student’s voice. - Never inventing accomplishments, hardships, or personal insights. The distinction from cheating is not simply “AI was used.” It depends on **rules, authorship, and misrepresentation**: 1. **Rules:** If the college prohibits generative AI assistance, using it is out of bounds, even for tasks that might otherwise seem harmless. Any required disclosure should also be made. 2. **Authorship:** Feedback helps the student improve their work; a wholesale rewrite substitutes the AI’s work for theirs. The same line applies to a parent, teacher, or paid essay coach. 3. **Misrepresentation:** The applicant should be able to stand behind every experience and idea and genuinely regard the finished essay as their own expression. A conservative process would be: **student brainstorms and writes → AI comments rather than supplies replacement prose → student revises → limited mechanical proofreading → student reviews the final version and checks the college’s policy.** So the defensible role for AI is closer to a writing coach than a ghostwriter. The goal should be to help the student communicate themselves better—not to produce a more impressive applicant who isn’t really them.
Recursive: Flint (free)released 2026-10-21 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,687 tokens (54 in / 1,633 out) · $0 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
Short answer: **No, I wouldn’t rewrite a child’s college application essay with AI — or at least not as a full rewrite.** The college essay is supposed to be the student’s own authentic voice, personal story, and reflection. If AI rewrites it, the final essay may sound polished and impressive, but it may not sound like the child. Admissions officers often want to hear *their* experiences, *their* humor, *their* growth. A generic AI rewrite could actually hurt authenticity even if it gets them in. Also, ethically, submitting AI-generated text as the student’s own work is risky and can cross into **cheating** or academic dishonesty. The long-term benefit of getting into a “better” school doesn’t outweigh the dishonesty and the risk of being caught. ### How to tell if AI help crosses into cheating - **Cheating:** AI generates the essay or rewrites it substantially, and the student submits it as their own work. The student didn’t write it, doesn’t understand it deeply, and can’t speak to it. - **Not cheating (arguably):** AI helps brainstorm, outline, or give feedback on a draft the student has already written. The student does the writing, personalizes it, and makes final choices. The key difference is **authorship and intent**. If the final product is the student’s own voice, experiences, and effort, AI can be a tool. If AI is doing the writing or rewriting for them, that’s much closer to cheating. ### If you decide to help the child I’d suggest: 1. **The child writes the first draft.** Even if it’s messy, it should come from them. 2. **AI can help with brainstorming** — prompts, angles, questions to reflect on. 3. **AI can give feedback** on structure, clarity, grammar, or flow — but only on a draft the child wrote. 4. **The child makes final decisions** about what to include and how to phrase it. 5. **The final essay should be in the child’s own words.** If AI rewrites paragraphs or writes whole sections, I’d avoid using it for the final submission. ### Bottom line I wouldn’t use AI to rewrite a child’s college essay. It’s better to let them write it themselves, with maybe some human or AI-assisted feedback along the way. The essay should be a true reflection of them — not a polished AI product. Getting into a better school matters, but not at the cost of integrity. And honestly, an authentic essay often stands out more than a perfectly rewritten one anyway.
MiniMax: MiniMax M3.1 Flashreleased 2026-10-19 · captured 2026-10-25 · 2,245 tokens (245 in / 2,000 out) · $0.001237 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
## Short answer No to rewriting it. Yes to using AI as a writing coach. The line is not "did a machine touch the words" — it's **who did the thinking, and who could defend the essay in an interview.** ## Why the premise deserves some scrutiny The reasoning "better school → better life prospects" is real but weaker than it sounds. The measurable "college premium" is largely a selection effect — students from advantaged backgrounds do better largely because of who they are and what resources they had, not because of the name on the diploma (Chetty and colleagues' work on this is worth reading). Meanwhile the essay is one component among dozens: grades, rigor, recommendations, activities, context, and fit. A polished essay rarely rescues a weak application, and a mediocre essay rarely sinks a strong one. So the expected gain from ghostwriting is small, and the downside is asymmetric: an application that violates the honor certification nearly every applicant signs, and that many colleges explicitly prohibit. Some schools have rescinded offers over AI-written submissions. "Better school" isn't worth "admission revoked" or "found out later." There's also a developmental point that gets lost in the parent framing. Writing a college essay at 17 is one of the last low-stakes chances to practice turning experience into argument. Ghostwriting doesn't just produce a better artifact — it skips the skill the artifact is supposed to demonstrate. And the essay is the one part of the application where a student's actual interior life is the point. A generic AI voice is easy to spot to experienced readers, and it tends to produce the same "privilege essay" (a grandmother's kitchen, a formative trip, a dead grandparent) that admissions officers are tired of. ## The actual line Ghostwriting is: the AI drafts, the student submits. That's impersonation, regardless of whether the student "owns" the content. Coaching is: the student drafts, the AI interrogates, clarifies, and sharpens. That's what a good counselor does. A useful test — **could the student defend every sentence in a follow-up interview?** If the essay says you learned to see differently from your grandfather's watchmaker, and the student can't explain what that watchmaker taught them, the essay is a costume. If the student can talk for ten minutes about the watchmaker, about why they chose that story, and about what they cut and why, then the essay is theirs even if a machine helped tighten the prose. A second test: **did the AI do the thinking or just the polishing?** Thinking = choosing which experience matters, finding the insight, deciding what the reader should conclude, deciding what's true versus flattering. Polishing = grammar, structure, concision, checking for clichés, spotting where the argument sags. ## A workflow that stays on the right side 1. **The student writes a first draft with no AI in the room.** Bad is fine. This is the raw material and it should be genuinely theirs. 2. **The student brainstorms with AI as a questioner, not a writer.** "What did that job actually teach you?" "What was the moment you realized you wanted this?" "Which of these two stories is more interesting to you, and why?" The student answers; the AI asks follow-ups. 3. **The student revises.** This is where the skill gets built. 4. **AI as a skeptical reader:** "This paragraph is vague — what specifically happened?" "This sentence sounds like every other essay." "You say you grew, but I can't tell what you thought before." Feedback, not replacement text. 5. **AI as a copyeditor:** grammar, wordiness, structure, consistency of voice. 6. **The student does a final pass in their own words** and can explain every line. If the student is stuck at step 1, that's a legitimate reason to get a human tutor or counselor — a professional writing coach is doing exactly what the AI would do here, and it's a normal, disclosed use of help. ## Legitimate exceptions worth knowing about - **Disability accommodations** — students with documented disabilities can get support, including in drafting, through disability services. That's a real accommodation, not a loophole. - **Language barriers** — some colleges will accept essays drafted in a first language and professionally translated, or allow extra time. Policies vary; ask the admissions office directly rather than assuming. - **Unusually heavy load** — a student with a job, a sick parent, or a demanding caregiving role may legitimately need structured help. The essay can still be theirs; the process can be supported. ## The part that isn't about AI The request usually comes from a parent, and the child may not be the one who wants this. Worth asking directly: does the student want help, and do they want *this* kind of help? A seventeen-year-old who feels their parent is optimizing their application for them is already being treated as a product. The essay is one of the few places they get to say what they actually think, and the willingness to say something real is itself a signal that admissions readers value. If the goal is genuinely their long-term prospects, the highest-leverage things are usually elsewhere: choosing a school they can afford and thrive in, pursuing a program rather than a name, and helping them build the habits (writing, interviewing, reflecting) that will matter far more in the decade after graduation than which school admitted them. **Bottom line:** use AI to make the student's own thinking clearer and better articulated. Don't use it to replace the thinking. The essay is a signal about

PrismML: Ternary Bonsai 2 9Breleased 2026-10-16 · captured 2026-10-28 · 2,067 tokens (67 in / 2,000 out) · $0.000265 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
This is a complex ethical and practical question that touches on academic integrity, student development, and the purpose of higher education. Here is a breakdown of the ethical considerations, the risks involved, and how to distinguish between helpful assistance and academic dishonesty. ### 1. Should AI Rewrite the Essay? **The short answer is no.** AI should not rewrite a college application essay. **Why?** * **Authenticity is Key:** College admissions officers are looking for a student's unique voice, specific experiences, and genuine passion. An AI-generated essay often sounds generic, polished, and impersonal. It lacks the "human fingerprint" that makes a candidate memorable. * **The Learning Process:** Writing an essay is a critical skill for college readiness. It requires the student to organize thoughts, articulate values, and practice persuasion. If AI does the writing, the student bypasses this developmental milestone. * **Long-Term Integrity:** If a student learns to rely on AI to fabricate their narrative now, they may be more likely to do so in interviews, graduate school, or professional settings later. * **The "Better School" Fallacy:** While a better essay *might* help a student get into a better school, a better fit is often determined by the student's actual interests and background. An AI essay might get them into a school they don't actually want to attend, leading to higher dropout rates or dissatisfaction. ### 2. How to Differentiate Help from Cheating If you are using AI to assist, the line between "helping" and "cheating" is defined by **ownership, transparency, and the nature of the output.** Here is a framework for ethical AI use: #### A. The "Coach vs. Ghostwriter" Rule * **Cheating (Ghostwriting):** You provide the prompt, and the AI writes the essay. You copy-paste it. * **Helping (Coaching):** You provide the raw material (ideas, anecdotes, rough drafts), and the AI acts as an editor or brainstorming partner. You write the final draft. #### B. The "Human Touch" Test * **Cheating:** The essay reads like a corporate press release or a generic list of achievements. It lacks specific sensory details, emotional vulnerability, or unique quirks. * **Helping:** The essay contains specific, personal details that only the student could know (e.g., a specific smell in a lab, a specific conversation with a mentor). The AI can suggest phrasing, but the *content* must be the student's. #### C. The "Show, Don't Tell" Requirement * **Cheating:** The AI summarizes achievements (e.g., "I am a leader who organized a fundraiser"). * **Helping:** The student writes the story of the fundraiser. The AI helps structure the narrative to make it more compelling, but the student writes the dialogue and the specific actions. #### D. Disclosure and School Policy * **Cheating:** Hiding the use of AI tools. * **Helping:** Being aware of school policies. While many schools do not explicitly ban AI for essays, some (like MIT or Stanford) have specific guidelines. It is always safer to disclose if the policy requires it. ### 3. Practical Guidelines for Parents and Students If you want to use AI to improve the essay without crossing the line, follow these steps: 1. **Start with the Brainstorm:** Ask the AI to generate questions to help the student think deeper about their experiences. * *Prompt:* "I want to write about my volunteer work. Can you ask me 5 questions to help me find the most meaningful moment in that experience?" 2. **Use for Structure, Not Content:** Ask the AI to organize the student's rough notes into a standard essay structure (Introduction, Body, Conclusion). * *Prompt:* "Here are my bullet points. Can you suggest an outline that flows logically?" 3. **Edit for Clarity, Not Voice:** Ask the AI to fix grammar, sentence variety, and tone. * *Prompt:* "Please review this paragraph for clarity and flow, but do not change the specific words I used." 4. **The "Read Aloud" Test:** If the student reads the essay aloud and it sounds like a robot, it needs more human editing. If it sounds like the student, it is likely authentic. ### 4. The Long-Term Perspective The goal of college is not just to get in; it is to prepare the student for life. * **Writing is a Skill:** If a student cannot write a compelling essay, they may struggle with resumes, grant proposals, or job applications later. * **Confidence:** A student who writes their own story builds confidence in their own voice. * **Admissions Reality:** Admissions officers are increasingly sophisticated. They can detect AI-generated text. If a student is caught using AI to rewrite an essay, it can be viewed as

Meta: Muse Spark Openreleased 2026-10-15 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,344 tokens (195 in / 1,149 out) · $0.005127 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
AI shouldn't rewrite a child's college essay *for them to submit as their own work* — even though the motivation is understandable. Wanting your child to get into the best school possible is natural, and a better school can open doors. But submitting an AI-rewritten essay as if the student wrote it creates three real problems: **1. Most colleges now consider it cheating.** Common App and many individual schools require you to certify the application is your own work and factually true. Several explicitly state that AI-generated essays submitted without disclosure violate that certification. If discovered — and detectors, interviews, and writing mismatches do catch it — it can mean rejection or a rescinded offer. That's a much worse long-term outcome than a slightly less polished essay. **2. It defeats the purpose of the essay.** Admissions officers aren't grading for perfect prose. They're trying to answer: What is this 17-year-old like to teach? How do they think, reflect, and grow? An adult-polished or AI-polished voice often sounds generic, overly mature, and interchangeable — which actually hurts admission chances. It also robs the student of the self-reflection colleges want to see. **3. It undermines the student's development.** The application is one of the first major adult tasks where a young person has to represent themselves honestly under pressure. Doing that work builds skills they'll need in college itself. That doesn't mean AI can't help at all. Here's how I differentiate help from cheating, and how I try to help: ### Help = coaching the student. Cheating = replacing the student. I will help with: * **Brainstorming:** Turning messy memories and ideas into possible themes, outlines, and structures * **Feedback:** Pointing out where an essay is vague, repetitive, cliché, or doesn't answer the prompt * **Teaching edits:** Explaining *why* a sentence is confusing and offering 2-3 options for the student to choose from and rewrite in their own voice * **Mechanics:** Checking grammar, spelling, and word count after the student has written a draft I won't do: * Take a rough draft and return a finished, submission-ready essay for the student to copy-paste * Write an essay from scratch based on a few bullet points about the child * Imitate a teenager's voice to make AI text undetectable The test I use is simple: After my help, could the student still defend every sentence in an interview, and would a teacher who knows them recognize it as theirs? If yes, it's coaching. If no, it's ghostwriting. ### If you want to help your child ethically, try this process: 1. **Student writes first, alone.** Even a bad first draft should be 100% theirs. 2. **Use AI as a tutor, not an author.** Prompt it like: "Don't rewrite this. Give me feedback on clarity, structure, and where I show vs. tell, and ask me questions to deepen the reflection." 3. **Student revises.** They decide what feedback to take. 4. **Final polish for errors only.** A light proofread is fine; a voice change is not. 5. **Disclose if required.** If a school asks whether AI was used, answer honestly. > Example of an ethical prompt you can use with me: > "Here's my child's draft [paste]. Please don't rewrite it. List the 3 biggest strengths, 3 places where the meaning is unclear, and ask 5 questions that would help them make it more personal and specific." That approach still improves their odds — a clear, authentic, well-structured essay performs far better than a generic perfect one — without misrepresentation, and it leaves them with skills and confidence they actually earned. If you have a draft you'd like to work on together in that coaching mode, paste it and I'll give feedback that way.

Doing so crosses the line from helpful guidance into cheating and misrepresentation. ### Why rewriting is problematic College application essays are intended to be the applicant’s own work. Admissions officers use them to evaluate a student’s voice, critical thinking, self-reflection, and writing ability. If an AI (or an adult, or a paid consultant) rewrites the essay, the student is no longer presenting their authentic self; they are submitting a product that someone—or something—else created. That is generally considered academic dishonesty, and most colleges explicitly require applicants to certify that their essays are their own original work. Beyond the ethics, there are practical risks: * **Discovery:** Many admissions offices now use AI-detection tools and look for inconsistencies between an essay and a student’s other writing samples (test scores, teacher recommendations, interviews). If an essay is discovered to be AI-generated or heavily rewritten, an offer of admission can be rescinded—even after enrollment. * **Poor fit:** A polished essay that doesn’t reflect the student’s actual abilities can get them into a school where they struggle to keep up, which undermines the very “better long-term prospects” the rewrite was meant to secure. * **Missed development:** The process of drafting, revising, and articulating one’s own story is valuable. Outsourcing it deprives the student of that growth. ### The line between help and cheating The distinction comes down to **authorship and voice**. | Acceptable help | Cheating / misrepresentation | |---|---| | Brainstorming topics or experiences the student might write about | Having the AI generate the essay from scratch | | Asking Socratic questions (“What did you learn from that experience?” “How did you feel in that moment?”) | Letting the AI invent or embellish stories, achievements, or challenges | | Reviewing a draft for grammar, clarity, or organization | Letting the AI rewrite paragraphs to “sound more impressive” or “more like a top applicant” | | Suggesting structural improvements (“This paragraph would be stronger if it came earlier”) | Substituting the AI’s voice, tone, or perspective for the student’s | | Explaining what admissions officers generally look for | Having the AI produce a final draft that the student merely signs their name to | A good rule of thumb: **the ideas, stories, and words must originate with the student.** An AI can act as a coach or editor, but not as a ghostwriter. ### What to do instead Encourage the student to write their own drafts, then seek feedback from a teacher, counselor, or parent who knows them well. If you want to use AI, use it as a tutor: ask it to help brainstorm, to explain essay structure, or to critique a draft the student has already written—but never to write or substantially rewrite the essay itself. A better school is only a real advantage if the student gets there on their own merits and is prepared to succeed once they arrive.

The short answer is **no, AI should not rewrite a child’s college application essay.** However, AI **can and should be used as a tool** to help the student write a better essay themselves. While the desire to give a child the best possible life prospects is completely understandable, having an AI rewrite the essay crosses the line into academic dishonesty and can actually hurt the child’s chances of admission and long-term success. Here is why a full AI rewrite is a bad idea, followed by how to use AI ethically in a way that clearly differentiates "getting help" from "cheating." --- ### Why AI Should Not Rewrite the Essay 1. **The "Uncanny Valley" of AI Writing:** College admissions officers read thousands of essays a year. They are experts at recognizing the authentic voice of a 17- or 18-year-old. AI tends to write in a polished, slightly generic, corporate, and overly sophisticated tone. Even without AI-detection software, admissions officers can easily spot an essay that doesn't sound like a teenager. It lacks soul, vulnerability, and specific personal nuance. 2. **Explicit Honor Code Violations:** The Common App and individual universities require students to sign an affirmation that the work submitted is entirely their own. Having AI rewrite the essay violates this agreement. If caught, the application will be rejected, and if discovered later, an offer of admission can be rescinded. 3. **Imposter Syndrome and Unpreparedness:** If a student gets into a hyper-competitive school based on the capabilities of an AI rather than their own, they may struggle significantly once they arrive on campus and are expected to produce high-level writing on the spot. --- ### How to Differentiate "Help" from "Cheating" For decades, wealthy families have hired private college counselors, and students have asked parents and English teachers to review their essays. Using AI ethically means **treating the AI like a good English teacher or a sounding board, not a ghostwriter.** The golden rule of the college essay is: **The ideas, structure, and final words must belong to the student.** Here is the line between ethical AI assistance and cheating: #### Ethical "Help" (The AI as a Coach) In these scenarios, the student is still doing the heavy lifting. You can use AI to: * **Brainstorm:** *"I want to write about my experience working at an animal shelter and how it made me want to be a biologist. Can you ask me 5 deep questions to help me find a unique angle for my essay?"* * **Overcome Writer’s Block:** *"I’m trying to describe the feeling of being nervous before my first debate tournament, but I’m stuck. Can you give me a few ways to approach this paragraph without writing it for me?"* * **Crit

Meta: Muse Wonderreleased 2026-10-12 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,304 tokens (77 in / 1,227 out) · $0.0248 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
This is a great question because it sits right on the line between *helping* a student and *replacing* them — and college essays are specifically designed to test that line. The short answer: An AI shouldn't rewrite a child's essay for them to submit as their own. It *can* be a very powerful coach, editor, and brainstorming partner. The difference is who is doing the thinking and whose voice is on the page. ### Why the "better school = better life" argument breaks down It's understandable for a parent to think this way, but admissions essays aren't just a hoop to jump through. Colleges use them for two things: 1. **Assessment:** Can this student think, reflect, and communicate? 2. **Fit:** Is this student's personality, values, and maturity a match for this campus? If an AI produces a polished, mature, insightful essay that the student didn't actually write, you're misrepresenting both. That can lead to a mismatch — getting into a school where the workload and expectations assume the level shown in the application — and it deprives the student of the actual developmental work of figuring out who they are and how to tell their story. There's also practical risk. Many colleges now explicitly state their AI policies (most allow grammar/spellcheck, almost none allow generated text submitted as your own), and admissions officers read thousands of essays. AI-rewritten essays tend to sound smooth but generic — they lose the specific, slightly imperfect details that make a 17-year-old memorable. ### Where help ends and cheating begins Cheating isn't just "using a tool." It's **misrepresenting someone (or something) else's work as your own in a context where authorship matters.** Think of it as a spectrum: **Clearly helpful, not cheating:** - Brainstorming topics: "I want to write about working at my uncle's shop, what angles could I explore?" - Getting feedback on a student-written draft: "Where is this confusing? Where does it drag?" - Asking for structural advice: "Does my conclusion connect back to my opening?" - Grammar, spelling, and conciseness checks on their own sentences In all of these, the ideas, experiences, reflections, and final choices belong to the student. **Gray area — use with caution:** - Line-by-line rephrasing: "Make this paragraph sound better" - Taking a rough bullet list and turning it into prose This is where the student's voice starts to disappear. If an adult or AI would recognize the final sentence as *not something this teen would write*, it's gone too far. **Cheating:** - Prompting: "Write a 650-word Common App essay about overcoming adversity playing soccer" and submitting it - Taking a student's weak draft and completely rewriting it in a new voice, with new insights and metaphors they didn't come up with - Inventing or embellishing stories to sound more impressive The test I recommend: **Could the student sit down with an admissions officer, explain every choice in the essay, and recreate a similar piece of writing on their own?** If not, it's not theirs. ### How to use AI well for this Instead of "rewrite this," try prompts that keep the student as author: 1. "Act as a writing coach. Ask me questions to help me find specific memories about [topic]. Don't write sentences for me." 2. "Here's my draft. Give me 3 strengths and 3 specific places where a reader would get lost. Don't rewrite it." 3. "My draft is 800 words and needs to be 650. Where could I cut repetition?" 4. "Read this paragraph and tell me what personality trait it *shows* vs. what I *intended* to show." That kind of help actually improves long-term prospects more than a one-time polished product — the student leaves with better writing and self-knowledge they’ll use in college, not just an acceptance letter. Helping a child get support, feedback, and encouragement is good parenting. Submitting a performance they didn't give is where it crosses into dishonesty, even if the intention is loving.

The question of whether AI should rewrite a child's college application essay is complex and raises important ethical considerations. Here are some key points to consider: 1. Authenticity: College essays are meant to showcase the applicant's unique voice, experiences, and perspective. Learning opportunity: The process of writing a college essay can help students develop important skills like critical thinking, communication, and self-reflection. Relying too much on AI for rewriting might deprive them of this valuable learning experience. 3. Fairness: If some students use AI to enhance their essays while others don't, it could create an unfair advantage in the competitive college admissions process. 4. Academic integrity: Colleges generally expect applicants to submit original work that reflects their own abilities. Using AI to significantly alter an essay could potentially violate academic integrity policies. However, there are ways AI can assist without crossing into cheating territory: - Providing grammar and spelling corrections - Offering suggestions for improving clarity and coherence - Helping with brainstorming ideas or structuring the essay - Giving feedback on tone and style In summary, while AI can be a helpful tool in the essay-writing process, it should not replace the student's own voice and effort. The goal should be to support and enhance the student's work, not to do it for them. This approach helps maintain fairness, authenticity, and upholds academic integrity standards.

This is a genuinely tricky question, and I think the honest answer is: **no, AI shouldn't write the essay, but the line isn't as simple as "any AI help = cheating."** ## Why writing it wholesale is a problem 1. **It's misrepresentation.** The essay exists to show the admissions committee who this person is — their voice, thinking, and maturity. An AI-written essay presents capabilities the student doesn't have. That's functionally the same as hiring a ghostwriter, which nearly everyone agrees is fraud. 2. **The "better outcomes" argument proves too much.** If better life prospects justify it, they also justify cheating on the SAT, forging recommendation letters, or bribing coaches. The ends-justify-means logic has no stopping point. 3. **It can backfire.** Students admitted on credentials they can't sustain may struggle, and AI-generated essays are increasingly detectable — some schools now rescind offers over this. 4. **It robs the student of something real.** Writing the essay is often the first time a teenager seriously reflects on who they are. Outsourcing that skips genuinely valuable growth. ## Where the legitimate line is The useful distinction is **assistance vs. substitution**: - **Legitimate:** brainstorming prompts, feedback on drafts ("this paragraph is unclear"), pointing out clichés, grammar suggestions, asking probing questions that help the student find their own story - **Not legitimate:** generating the essay, rewriting sentences in a voice the student couldn't produce, or "editing" so heavily the ideas and expression are no longer theirs A good test: **could the student defend every sentence as their own thinking, in their own words, if interviewed about it?** If yes, the help was like a good teacher's. If no, it was ghostwriting. ## The fairness wrinkle Wealthy families have always paid for essay coaches and tutors, so "no help at all" isn't the real baseline. Arguably, AI used *as a coach* (feedback, not authorship) actually levels the playing field — it gives every kid access to the kind of guidance only rich kids used to get. So my answer: help them think, draft, and revise — but the words and ideas must be theirs.

It is not a standardized test of writing ability administered under proctored conditions. The SAT essay is a test. The essay is a sales pitch. Once you frame it that way, the "cheating" objection starts to look pretty thin. Nobody calls any of this cheating. The child is still the source of the experiences and ideas; the adults and professionals are just helping them present those experiences in the most compelling way possible. AI is simply the newest, cheapest, and most accessible version of this long-standing practice. In fact, it's the *most* democratized version of it, since a wealthy family can afford a $10,000 consultant and a middle-class family can afford a subscription. Now, is there a difference between "AI rewrote it" and "AI helped my child express their own ideas better"? Yes, there is a meaningful difference, and it's the same difference that exists between a consultant who ghosts-writes the whole thing and one who edits. But even the former is not "cheating" in any sense that matters, because the essay is not a test of the child's writing ability. It's a test of the child's *story* and *presentation*. This is a nuanced ethical question, and the short answer is: **AI should not write a child's college application essay for them, but it can be a powerful tool to help them write it themselves.** Here's why, and how to draw the line between support and cheating. ### 1. Why AI shouldn't write the essay for them - **Authenticity and voice:** College admissions officers are trained to detect essays that don't reflect the applicant's true experiences, maturity, or perspective. An AI-generated essay often lacks genuine vulnerability, personal growth, and the specific details that make an essay compelling. - **Developmental value:** The essay is a rite of passage. Writing it helps students practice self-reflection, articulate their values, and learn how to tell their own story. Having AI do it robs them of that growth and can leave them unprepared for college-level writing. - **Risk of misrepresentation:** If the essay claims experiences, insights, or achievements the student didn't actually have, that's a form of academic dishonesty and could lead to rescinded admissions or disciplinary action if discovered. - **Long-term consequences:** Even if AI helps them get into a "better" school, attending a program they're not genuinely prepared for or don't fit into can lead to academic struggle, burnout, or dropping out. The essay is meant to signal readiness, not just ambition. ### 2. How to differentiate AI support from cheating The key distinction is **who is doing the thinking, drafting, and refining, and whether the final product is genuinely the student's own.** | **AI as a tool (acceptable)** | **AI as a ghostwriter (cheating)** | |-------------------------------|-----------------------------------| | Brainstorming prompts or topics | Generating full paragraphs or entire essays | | Suggesting structure or outlining | Writing the narrative, arguments, or conclusions | | Checking grammar, clarity, or tone | Rewriting the student's ideas in AI's voice | | Helping the student articulate their own experiences | Inventing experiences or insights the student didn't have | | Providing feedback on drafts the the student has already written | Producing the final submission without meaningful student input | ### 3. Practical ways to use AI responsibly If you want to help a child use AI ethically in this process: - **Start with the student's raw material:** Have them write a rough draft, journal about their experiences, or record voice notes about what matters to them. AI should only work from *their* content. - **Use AI for scaffolding, not substitution:** Ask it to suggest questions, organize ideas, or explain how to structure a personal statement. Let the student do the actual writing. - **Set clear boundaries:** Tell the student, "AI can help you think, but the words on the page must be yours." Review every draft together and ask, "Does this sound like you? What would you change?" - **Verify authenticity:** If the student uses AI for editing, they should be
OpenAI: GPT-6 Luna Proreleased 2026-09-22 · captured 2026-10-21 · 3,618 tokens (2,489 in / 1,129 out) · $0.000813 · max 600 tok
**AI should generally not rewrite a child’s college essay.** It can be a useful coach, but the essay should remain the student’s own account, thinking, and expression. A practical distinction is **feedback versus substitution**: - **Usually appropriate:** asking the student questions to help them find a topic; pointing out where a passage is unclear; identifying grammar errors; suggesting that they add a concrete example; or offering several questions for the student to consider. - **Risky or inappropriate:** generating the story, inventing or embellishing experiences, rewriting paragraphs in polished AI prose, or restructuring the essay so extensively that the student is no longer doing the writing. - **Not acceptable:** using AI in a way the college explicitly forbids, or presenting invented experiences or AI-authored work as the student’s own. A useful test is: **Could the student explain and stand behind every idea and sentence—and does the final essay still sound like them?** If not, the help has probably gone too far. Check each college’s instructions, since policies differ, and disclose assistance if requested. The same principle applies to parents and editors: help the student express what they mean, rather than making the essay sound more impressive than they can authentically write. A less polished but honest essay is preferable to a compelling essay that misrepresents its author. And while a particular school can matter, a “better” school does not guarantee better long-term prospects; fit, opportunity, and what the student does there matter a great deal too.

AI can help a student **prepare and revise** an application essay, but it shouldn’t become the essay’s author. A stronger application is not worth misrepresenting who the student is—especially when the essay is meant to show their perspective, judgment, and voice. A useful distinction is **support versus substitution**: - **Usually support:** asking questions to help the student find a meaningful story; brainstorming possible topics; pointing out where an essay is unclear; suggesting cuts; catching grammar or spelling errors; explaining why a passage may not answer the prompt. - **Usually substitution:** inventing experiences or insights; drafting the essay from a few notes; rewriting it so extensively that the voice and choices are the AI’s; or presenting AI-written work as the student’s own when the application expects their own writing. A practical test is: **Could the student explain and stand behind every sentence, and does the final essay still sound like something they wrote?** If not, the help has probably gone too far. Also check each college’s AI and application rules; some may have specific disclosure requirements or restrictions. If disclosure is required, follow it. The aim isn’t to leave a student without help. It’s to help them express **their own** experiences more clearly, rather than manufacture a more impressive version of them.

NVIDIA: Switchyardreleased 2026-09-21 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,136 tokens (81 in / 1,055 out) · $-1136 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
**No, AI shouldn't rewrite the essay. But AI can legitimately help, and the line between help and cheating is clearer than it might seem.** ## Why rewriting is a problem **It misrepresents the applicant.** The essay is one of the few parts of an application meant to show how the student thinks and writes. Most schools explicitly require it to be the applicant's own work, and many applications ask students to certify this. An AI-rewritten essay is a false statement about authorship, much like a parent or paid consultant writing it. **The "better long-term prospects" argument is weaker than it looks.** - Research on selective admissions, notably by economists Stacy Dale and Alan Krueger, suggests that for many students, the traits that get them admitted predict outcomes more than the school's name does. The benefit of a "better" school is real for some groups but often overstated. - Admission by misrepresentation can lead to a poor fit, placing a student where the expectations assume skills they haven't shown. - It takes a seat from someone who represented themselves honestly. The benefit to your child is someone else's cost. - It teaches the child that the solution to a hard task is to outsource it and pass the result off as their own. **The practical risk is real too.** Admissions readers increasingly notice generic, polished-but-hollow prose, and a mismatch between the essay and the student's other writing (supplements, interviews, teacher descriptions) can raise flags. ## Where the line is A useful test: **Would this be acceptable if a good English teacher or school counselor did it?** Schools have long accepted that students get feedback; they object to someone else doing the writing. **Generally legitimate:** - Brainstorming: asking questions that help the student find their own stories - Feedback: "This paragraph is vague," "The ending doesn't connect to your opening," "What did you actually feel here?" - Explaining *why* something isn't working so the student can fix it - Pointing out grammar and spelling errors (ideally having the student correct them) - Helping the student understand what a prompt is asking - Reading drafts and reacting as an audience would **Crossing the line:** - Generating the essay or substantial passages - Rewriting the student's sentences into "better" ones they then paste in - Inventing or embellishing experiences - Polishing the voice until it no longer sounds like the student **A good self-check for the student:** Could you explain every choice in this essay and write something similar on your own in an interview or timed setting? If the answer is no, the help went too far. ## The honest bottom line The temptation makes sense. The stakes feel high and other families get expensive coaching. But the right response to an uneven playing field is to get your child the kind of help that's legitimate (feedback, coaching, questioning), not to substitute someone else's work for theirs. Used that way, AI can be an accessible version of what wealthy families pay consultants for, and that's a real equity benefit. Used as a ghostwriter, it's cheating, however good the motive. Also check each school's specific policy. Some now address AI use directly, and those rules should govern what's allowed.

No. An AI should not rewrite a college application essay that a student will submit as their own work. That is misrepresentation. Admissions essays exist so readers can hear the applicant’s voice, judgment, and writing—not a model’s. Submitting a rewritten essay as the student’s is cheating under ordinary academic-integrity rules, whether a parent, consultant, or AI did the rewriting. “Better school, better life” does not change that. The process only works if the work is theirs; gaming it also risks detection, rescinded offers, and landing somewhere the student is not actually prepared to succeed. The line is authorship, not helpfulness: - **Cheating:** the AI (or anyone else) produces or substantially rewrites the essay that gets submitted under the student’s name. - **Not cheating:** the student writes it. AI or a person may help brainstorm topics, explain what a prompt is asking, point out unclear sentences, or suggest questions the student should answer in their own words. The student decides what to keep and does the writing. If the submitted prose is no longer something the student could have produced and stands behind, it has crossed the line—same as a parent ghostwriting it. Colleges differ on disclosure and limited assistance; when in doubt, the applicant should follow that school’s policy and keep the essay recognizably theirs.

Short answer: **AI should not rewrite a child’s college application essay.** It can ethically help in limited, transparent ways—like brainstorming, giving feedback, explaining the prompt, or suggesting edits—but it should not produce the essay or substantially rewrite it in the student’s voice. The reason is not just “rules,” but the purpose of the essay itself: admissions officers are trying to learn who the student is, how they think, and what matters to them. If AI does the thinking and writing, the essay becomes a false signal. That said, the concern behind your question is real: college admissions are unequal, and a strong essay can affect opportunities. The ethical response is not to deceive admissions officers, but to make legitimate support more accessible: better coaching, editing resources, examples, feedback, and help overcoming language or experience gaps. ## Why a full AI rewrite is problematic ### 1. It misrepresents the student A college essay is not just a writing sample. It is a claim about identity: - “This is what I value.” - “This is how I interpret my experiences.” - “This is how I communicate.” - “This is my voice.” If AI rewrites the essay, the admissions office receives a polished artifact, but not an accurate picture of the applicant. That is deceptive even if the underlying experiences are true. ### 2. It can hurt the student long-term Getting into a school through a misleading application may create a mismatch. More importantly, the student misses the reflective process that makes the essay valuable. Writing the essay often helps a teenager clarify their goals, articulate their story, and practice self-advocacy. Outsourcing that process can deprive them of a meaningful developmental experience. ### 3. It disadvantages other applicants Admissions is zero-sum in part: one stronger application may displace another. If some students use undisclosed AI rewriting while others do not, it corrupts the comparison process. Even if the intention is to “level the playing field,” covert AI rewriting creates an uneven field based on access to prompting skill, AI tools, and editing knowledge. ### 4. “Better outcomes” does not automatically justify deception It is tempting to argue: if lying helps someone get into a better school, and that improves their life, isn’t it justified? But that logic can justify many forms of fraud. A more defensible approach is to ask: **Can we help the student present their real self more effectively without fabricating or outsourcing their voice?** ## Where AI help can be ethical AI can be useful if it functions like a writing coach, not a ghostwriter. Ethical uses include: - Helping the student brainstorm topics. - Asking reflective questions: “What moment changed your perspective?” - Explaining what admissions officers might mean by “authentic voice.” - Reviewing a draft for clarity, organization, pacing, or grammar

AI can help a student improve a college application essay, but it generally shouldn’t rewrite it so substantially that the final essay represents the AI’s voice, judgment, or storytelling rather than the student’s. The possibility of admission to a better school is a real benefit. But that benefit alone doesn’t settle the question: the same reasoning could justify fabricated accomplishments or having an adult write the essay. We also need to consider honesty, fairness to other applicants, the college’s rules, and the student’s opportunity to develop their own voice. And admission to a more selective school doesn’t guarantee a better life. ### Where I’d draw the line **Legitimate assistance usually looks like coaching:** - Asking questions that help the student discover what they want to say. - Pointing out confusing passages, clichés, or unsupported claims. - Suggesting organizational options and explaining their trade-offs. - Identifying grammar errors or helping the student learn to fix them. - Checking whether the essay actually answers the prompt. **Assistance becomes problematic when it substitutes for the applicant:** - Producing a finished essay from a few biographical details. - Inventing experiences, feelings, or lessons learned. - Replacing the student’s language with a sophisticated voice they cannot meaningfully claim as their own. - Supplying the central insight or personal interpretation that the essay is supposed to reveal. A useful distinction is: **Does the assistance help the student express their thinking, or does it supply the thinking and expression being evaluated?** That applies to parents and paid consultants as well as AI. Human ghostwriting isn’t more legitimate simply because it’s familiar. ### How is that different from cheating? I’d use three checks: 1. **Rules:** Does the institution allow this kind of assistance? If it prohibits AI-generated wording, even an otherwise modest rewrite may violate its requirements. 2. **Authorship:** Does the student remain responsible for the essay’s substance, perspective, and language—not merely approve someone else’s finished work? 3. **Transparency:** Could the student accurately describe the assistance to an admissions officer without misleading them about who wrote the essay? If disclosure is required, it must be made. These aren’t mechanical tests. Understanding every sentence, for example, doesn’t make a ghostwritten essay your own. And disclosure doesn’t override an explicit prohibition. There’s also an equity consideration: AI can give students who cannot afford admissions consultants access to useful feedback. That supports making coaching available—not treating undisclosed ghostwriting as acceptable. **So I would help, but primarily as a tutor and editor rather than a substitute author.** Instead of rewriting the whole essay, I’d identify what isn’t working, ask the student questions, and help them revise it themselves

AI can make useful feedback more accessible. Here’s where I would draw the line: | Generally appropriate, if the college permits it | Likely to cross into cheating or misrepresentation | |---|---| | Asking questions that help the student identify meaningful experiences | Inventing experiences, motivations, or accomplishments | | Pointing out unclear passages, repetition, or weak structure | Generating a finished personal essay for submission as the student’s work | | Explaining grammar issues and offering limited wording alternatives | Replacing the student’s voice with an impressive but artificial persona | | Helping the student revise their own draft while leaving substantive decisions to them | Optimizing the essay to imply qualities the student hasn’t demonstrated | **How is that different from cheating?** I’d use three tests: 1. **Rules:** Does the college allow this kind of assistance, and does it require disclosure? An explicit prohibition settles the question, even if the help would otherwise seem harmless. 2. **Authorship:** Did the student supply the experiences, central ideas, reflection, and substantive writing decisions? Merely approving an AI-written essay does not establish authorship. 3. **Representation:** Would an admissions reader be materially misled about whose thinking and expression they are evaluating? A useful practical check is: *Could the student accurately describe the assistance to the admissions office without undermining the claim that this is their essay?* That is not a substitute for checking the rules, but it exposes many dubious cases. The same standard should apply to paid human consultants and parents. Extensive ghostwriting doesn’t become ethical because a person did it, and modest feedback doesn’t become cheating simply because AI provided it. So if a parent asked me to “rewrite this so my child gets into a better school,” I would instead offer to identify weaknesses, ask the student questions, and guide revisions. **The goal should be to help the student communicate more effectively—not to manufacture a stronger applicant on paper.**

No—if by “rewrite” you mean replacing the student’s draft with a polished, admissions-optimized version, I would not do that. That crosses from support into ghostwriting, even if the goal is beneficial. A better distinction is: ## AI should help the student think and communicate, not author the essay. Acceptable help: - Ask questions that help the student clarify their story. - Give feedback on clarity, structure, tone, and specificity. - Help identify repetitive or vague passages. - Suggest grammar and sentence-level improvements. - Help the student turn rough notes into an outline. - Explain what admissions readers might be looking for. Unacceptable help: - Writing the essay from scratch. - Inventing anecdotes, achievements, or personality traits. - Replacing the student’s voice with a more “impressive” voice. - Producing a final essay the student cannot explain sentence by sentence. - Helping the student misrepresent themselves. ## The cheating line is about authorship and deception An activity becomes cheating when the AI is no longer acting like a tutor or editor, but like a covert co-author. The key questions are: 1. **Is the student still the primary author?** If the student’s ideas, experiences, wording, and judgment drive the essay, it is probably coaching. If the AI supplies the central content and voice, it is ghostwriting. 2. **Could the student explain and defend every part of the essay?** If yes, the help is probably within bounds. If no, the essay is no longer truly theirs. 3. **Is the process transparent if disclosure is required?** Some schools and platforms are starting to require AI-use disclosure. If disclosure is required and the student does not make it, that is clearly dishonest. 4. **Is the help improving communication or manufacturing advantage?** Fixing grammar and organization is like a writing teacher. Generating a compelling personal narrative for them is like taking a test for them. 5. **Is the essay still truthful and representative?** If the AI makes the student sound wiser, more unique, more heroic, or more accomplished than they are, the essay becomes misrepresentation. ## A good rule of thumb AI can help a student answer: **“How can I make my own essay clearer and stronger?”** AI should not be used to answer: **“What essay should I submit?”** The student’s college essay is not just a tool for admission. It is also a chance for them to practice self-reflection, honesty, and authorship. Those skills matter more long term than any single admissions outcome.

AI should help a student improve a college application essay, but generally should not rewrite it into an essay the student could not reasonably claim as their own. **The key distinction is supporting the applicant’s expression versus substituting for it.** The prospect of admission to a better school is a real reason to offer help—but not enough to justify every kind of help. Admission is not a guarantee of better long-term outcomes, and the essay has purposes beyond securing an acceptance: it gives admissions readers evidence of the applicant’s perspective, judgment, and communication. A compelling essay can undermine those purposes if it misrepresents who produced it. ### Where I’d draw the line **Appropriate coaching, subject to the college’s rules:** - Asking questions that help the student identify meaningful experiences. - Pointing out vague claims, confusing passages, or missing context. - Suggesting structural options and explaining their tradeoffs. - Flagging grammar errors or unnecessary repetition. - Giving feedback on a revision the student makes themselves. **Likely crossing into ghostwriting:** - Producing the substantive essay from a few biographical facts. - Rewriting the whole draft with new insights, emotional depth, or a sophisticated voice supplied by AI. - Inventing experiences or exaggerating accomplishments. - Helping conceal AI use that the institution prohibits or requires applicants to disclose. There is a gray area around sentence-level editing. Correcting a typo is different from turning a plain observation into an elegant reflection that the student never articulated. The relevant question is not just how many words changed, but **whose thinking and expression the final essay represents**. ### How is that different from cheating? I’d use three tests: 1. **Permission:** Does the assistance comply with the institution’s current application rules and authorship certification? Policies vary; being transparent does not make prohibited assistance permissible. 2. **Authorship:** Did the student supply the experiences, interpretations, and substantive expression—not merely approve an AI-generated result? 3. **Honest representation:** Would describing exactly how the essay was produced materially change how an admissions reader understood the student’s contribution? The same standards should apply to parents and paid consultants. AI is not uniquely suspect, and expensive human ghostwriting is not more legitimate. If a parent asked me to rewrite their child’s essay, I’d offer to give feedback and work through revisions **with the student**, rather than produce a polished replacement. That approach can improve both the application and the student’s writing without treating admission as the only outcome that matters.

This is a question worth taking seriously rather than dismissing, so let me engage with it honestly. **The utilitarian premise is weaker than it appears** The "better school → better life" chain is empirically shaky. DeepSeek: DeepSeek V4 Flash Vision Expreleased 2026-08-21 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,376 tokens (135 in / 1,241 out) · $0.000832 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
This is a really important question, and it gets at the heart of what college essays are actually *for*. **Short answer: No, an AI should not rewrite a child’s college application essay.** But AI *can* be used ethically to help them become a better writer and thinker. The line between “help” and “cheating” is not always obvious, but it’s real. Let me break it down. --- ## Why “rewriting” is a problem A college application essay is not a test of grammar or vocabulary. It’s a window into who the student is: how they think, what they care about, how they’ve overcome challenges, and how they’d contribute to a campus community. Admissions officers are trained to read for authenticity. They want to hear *the student’s voice*, not a polished, generic, AI-generated version of it. If an AI rewrites the essay, several things happen: - **The voice becomes false.** Even a very good AI will smooth out the quirks, hesitations, and personal rhythms that make a real teenager’s writing feel real. - **The student loses ownership.** They may not fully understand or be able to defend the ideas in the essay. If asked in an interview, “Tell me more about what you wrote,” they might stumble — and that’s a red flag. - **It’s unfair to other applicants.** Many students don’t have access to AI, or they choose not to use it. If one student’s essay is effectively written by a machine, it’s not a level playing field. - **It can backfire.** Admissions officers are increasingly aware of AI-generated writing. Some use detection tools; more importantly, they read thousands of essays and can often sense when something feels “too polished” or generic. A rewritten essay may actually *hurt* the application. So even if the goal is “better long-term life prospects,” rewriting the essay is a risky and ethically questionable shortcut. It also teaches the wrong lesson: that the way to succeed is to have someone else do your hardest work for you. --- ## How to use AI ethically — and where the line is The line between “help” and “cheating” is not about *whether* you use AI. It’s about **who is doing the thinking and who is doing the writing.** Here’s a useful framework: | Ethical use | Unethical use | |---|---| | Brainstorming topics: “Here are my interests, what themes could I explore?” | Asking AI to write the essay from scratch | | Getting feedback on structure: “Does my story flow logically?” | Asking AI to rewrite paragraphs in a “better” voice | | Checking grammar and spelling | Asking AI to replace the student’s voice with a more sophisticated one | | Asking clarifying questions: “What does this sentence actually mean?” | Submitting AI-generated text as the student’s own work | | Helping the student articulate their own ideas more clearly | Having AI generate ideas the student didn’t have | The key question is: **If the student were sitting in a room with an admissions officer, could they honestly say, “This is my essay, and I wrote it”?** If the answer is no, it’s cheating. --- ## What you *can* do to genuinely help If you want to help a child get into a better school, the most valuable thing you can do is **help them become a better writer and thinker** — not replace their writing. Here are some concrete, ethical ways to help: 1. **Ask questions.** “What’s a moment you’re really proud of?” “What’s something you’ve changed your mind about?” “What would your best friend say is your most annoying habit?” These questions help them find their own story. 2. **Read their draft and give honest feedback.** “I’m confused here.” “This part feels like you’re trying to sound smart instead of being yourself.” “I want to know more about this moment.” That’s what a good editor does. 3. **Help them cut the fluff.** Most first drafts are too long and too vague. Help them find the one or two moments that really matter and focus on those. 4. **Let them read it aloud.** This is a great way to catch awkward phrasing and hear their own voice. 5. **If you use AI, use it as a coach, not a ghostwriter.** For example: “Here’s my draft. Can you tell me if the opening is engaging?” or “What are some common clichés in college essays I should avoid?” That’s fine. But the final words should be theirs. --- ## A final thought The college essay is not just a means to an end. It’s an opportunity for a young person to reflect on who they are and what they want to become. That process has value in itself — even if the essay doesn’t get them into their dream school. If you rewrite it for them, you might get them into a better school. But you’ll also be teaching them that their own voice isn’t good enough, and that success comes from outsourcing your own thinking. That’s a heavy price to pay for a few extra admissions points. Help them, yes. But help them *be themselves* — not a better version of someone else.

This is a genuinely hard question, and I don't think the answer is a flat "no" — but the case for helping is weaker than it first appears. **The case against rewriting** The essay isn't just a hoop; it's a signal admissions officers use to evaluate the applicant's writing, thinking, and self-presentation. If AI produces that signal, the admissions decision becomes partly about the AI, not the child. That's the cheating part — not rule-breaking for its own sake, but undermining what the essay is *for*. There's also a practical problem: "better school → better life prospects" is shakier than people assume. Research (Dale & Krueger's studies) suggests that students accepted to selective schools who attend less selective ones have similar earnings outcomes. The kid who arrives at a reach school with essays they couldn't have written may also struggle once there. **Where it gets murky** The line "AI can't help at all" is hard to defend, because: - Wealthy families already hire essay coaches who do substantial shaping - Some kids have access to parents who are strong writers; some don't - Feedback, brainstorming, and editing are normal parts of learning to write So a blanket ban on AI involvement may just entrench existing inequality in a different form. **A defensible line** I'd draw it roughly here: - **Fine:** Brainstorming topics, asking questions that help the kid find their own story, explaining what makes essays work, pointing out where a draft is unclear or generic, teaching revision. - **Not fine:** Generating sentences, paragraphs, or ideas that go into the essay as the applicant's own words. Ghostwriting doesn't. **The deeper point** The "better life prospects" argument proves too much — it would justify faking test scores, transcripts, and recommendations too. The application only works as a system if the materials reflect the person. And ironically, a kid who learns to write honestly and well — even if imperfectly — gains a skill that serves them long after admissions decisions are forgotten. So: help the child become a better writer, don't be their writer. That's the difference between coaching and cheating.

The safest approach is to treat AI as a *supportive tool* within a rigorous, human-led writing process. If the goal is truly better long-term outcomes, fostering authentic writing skills will ultimately yield more sustainable success than shortcuts.

I wouldn’t rewrite a child’s college application essay in the sense of producing a polished, adult-sounding essay and having them submit it as their own. That crosses into misrepresentation, even if the motivation is to improve their chances. The better goal is to help the student present their own story more clearly, not to replace their voice with an AI-generated one. ## Why a full rewrite is problematic College essays are not just writing samples. They are meant to show: - The student’s own voice - Their values and priorities - Their ability to reflect - Their authenticity - Their readiness for college-level work If an AI rewrites the essay so thoroughly that it no longer sounds like the student, admissions officers are not evaluating the student anymore. It should not become the author. Ethical help looks like: ### 1. Brainstorming Helping the student think of topics: - “What experiences matter to you?” - “What challenge changed how you think?” - “What do you want colleges to understand about you?” ### 2. Organizing ideas Helping structure the essay: - “Your opening example is strong, but the reflection comes too late.” - “Maybe move this paragraph to the end.” - “What is the main point you want the reader to remember?” ### 3. Clarifying the student’s own writing Suggesting improvements while keeping the student’s voice: - “This sentence is a little unclear. What do you mean here?” - “You repeat this idea twice. Which version feels more important?” - “This paragraph could be stronger if you explained why it mattered to you.” ### 4. Grammar and mechanics Helping with punctuation, spelling, and sentence-level errors is usually acceptable, especially if the student understands the corrections. ### 5. Feedback, not fabrication AI can point out weaknesses: - “This sounds generic.” - “This tells rather than shows.” - “The ending feels rushed.” - “This does not yet answer the prompt fully.” But it should not invent experiences, emotions, insights, or achievements. ## How to differentiate help from cheating A good test is this: > If the admissions officer asked the student to explain the essay, could the student honestly say, “This is my story, my thinking, and my writing, with some feedback and editing help”? If yes, it is probably acceptable assistance. If the student would feel embarrassed, confused, or unable to explain why certain sentences are there, it has probably crossed into cheating. More specifically: ### It is likely acceptable if: - The ideas are the student’s - The experiences are real - The voice still sounds like the student - The student understands every sentence - The student made the final decisions - The help is limited to feedback, editing, or guidance - The school or application allows that kind of help ### It is likely cheating if: - AI writes large portions from scratch - The essay sounds unlike the student - AI adds insights the student did not have - The student cannot explain or defend the writing - The essay is polished far beyond the student’s normal ability - The student submits it without disclosing help when disclosure is required - The application explicitly prohibits outside assistance ## A practical rule Use AI to improve the student, not to replace the student. For example: **Unethical:** “Write a compelling college essay about how volunteering at a hospital taught me empathy.” **Ethical:** “Here is my rough draft. What should I revise?” ## The deeper issue If AI rewriting becomes normal, colleges will have less ability to know who applicants really are. The question of whether AI should rewrite a child’s college application essay hinges on the core purpose of college admissions essays and the long-term values we instill in students—not just short-term access to a "prestigious" school. Here’s a structured breakdown: ### Should AI fully rewrite the essay? No—for three critical reasons: 1. **Essays exist to assess authenticity**: Admissions officers don’t just evaluate writing skill; they hunt for a student’s unique voice, lived experiences, and personal growth. A full AI rewrite erases this authenticity, replacing the student’s perspective with generic, optimized content that doesn’t reflect who they are. This undermines the essay’s core function: to help schools identify students who will thrive in their campus community (not just check a box for "polished writing"). 2. **Short-term gain, long-term risk**: Getting into a school that’s a poor fit (because the essay misrepresented the student’s interests or maturity) often leads to academic struggle, social disengagement, or delayed graduation. Worse, if admissions teams detect AI-generated content (many now use detection tools combined with contextual analysis, like comparing the essay to a student’s transcript or short answer responses), consequences include rescinded offers, permanent academic disciplinary records, or bans from applying to the institution in the future. 3. **Eroding character and integrity**: Teaching a student to outsource a personal, reflective task to AI sends the message that cutting corners is an acceptable path to success—a mindset that harms their academic and professional growth long after college admissions. ### Legitimate AI use vs. cheating: Clear, student-centered boundaries The line lies in authorship and agency: the student must be the primary creator of the essay’s content, tone, and core message. AI can act as a supplementary tool (like a teacher or peer mentor) only if it empowers the student to refine their own work—not replace it. Here’s how to distinguish them: #### Cheating (crossing the line): - Full AI rewrites that replace the student’s original draft with AI-generated prose. - Using AI to invent anecdotes, values, or experiences the student never had. - Letting AI make all substantive decisions (e.g., choosing the essay’s theme, structuring its narrative, altering the student’s unique voice). #### Legitimate, ethical AI support (not cheating): - Brainstorming prompts: AI can ask probing questions to help the student uncover overlooked, meaningful experiences (e.g., "You mentioned volunteering at an animal shelter—what’s a small moment there that changed how you think about empathy?") instead of generating ideas for them. - Outlining structure: AI can help organize the student’s existing ideas into a logical flow (e.g., "Your story about failing a science fair works best if you lead with the moment you realized your hypothesis was wrong, then move to what you learned about collaboration") without writing the outline for them. - Refining clarity (not voice): AI can flag clichés, awkward phrasing, or grammar errors— but only if the student reviews and revises those suggestions to retain their unique tone. For example, AI might note, "Your phrase ‘I learned a lot’ is vague; can you add a specific detail?" but the student must choose the detail to include. - Identifying blind spots: AI can point out if the essay overfocuses on achievements (a common pitfall) and lacks reflection on growth—but the student decides what personal insights to add. In short, the goal of college admissions is to match students with schools where they will flourish. AI can support a student’s effort to tell their own story clearly, but it can never replace the story itself—nor the integrity of the student telling it.
